Yes. So for an AI-based attack that is specifically a cybersecurity attack, there are notification requirements that already exist under law: 72 hours for a confirmed breach, 24 hours for a breach related to extortion and 30 days for victim notification. So there are plans in place for incident response in the event of a cyber attack against any critical infrastructure owned by the City, and that includes if that attack is perpetrated by an artificial intelligence system.

**(06:28:31)**



Thanks. And on the misinformation front, two quick points. One: I think that is a real risk and I appreciate the Council Member raising it. It is one of the reasons that we invest very heavily in backup systems. Several months ago, a very well-known and established notification provider had a seven-hour global outage where notifications were not able to be sent out. That is something that we take very seriously, which is why for over a decade now New York City Emergency Management has invested in backup systems. As you went across the country, you saw many large jurisdictions saying to their constituents, "You know, sign up for this group or follow us on social media if you want to learn tonight." In New York City, we do not have to do that because we had a backup system that continued to work, so that disruption was averted.

As far as misinformation generated by AI, that is a valid concern, which is why we recommend that New Yorkers follow us on our official channels, which are verified through Notify NYC and notify.gov. You can sign up by short code or download the Notify NYC mobile app that we operate. Thank you so much.

I now open the hearing for public testimony. I remind members of the public that this is a formal government proceeding and that decorum shall be observed at all times. As such, members of the public should remain seated to testify. No video recording or photography is allowed from the witness table. Further, members of the public may not present audio or video recordings as testimony, but they may submit transcripts of such recordings to the Sergeant at Arms for inclusion in the hearing record. If you wish to speak at today's hearing and you have not done so, please fill out an appearance card with the Sergeant at Arms and wait to be recognized. Once recognized, you will have two minutes to speak on today's hearing topic, which is examining the risks posed by artificial intelligence and proposed legislation. If you have a written statement or additional written materials you wish to submit for the record, please provide a copy of that testimony to the Sergeant at Arms. You may also mail written testimony to testimony@council.nyc.gov within 72 hours of this hearing. Audio and video recordings will not be accepted.

The first panel includes Nate Source, Dr. Julia Six, Diana Vich, Herman Ahmed, Yaque Hasooh, Mackenzie Arnold, Nathan Sheared and Ana Mares. When you speak, please identify yourself for the record and if I messed up your name, I apologize — that does happen. We are also asking folks to please remain within the two-minute time limit. Thank you.

**(06:32:20)**



You may begin. Please stand on the side of the dais and just identify yourself. Good evening, Speaker Menin and members of the Council. My name is Imran Ahmed. I am the founder of the Center for Countering Digital...

**(06:32:43)**



You may continue. Go ahead.

**(06:32:47)**



So after my colleague was murdered ten years ago by a man radicalized online, I began studying the design of social media platforms to understand how they generate risks and threats to real people. Today we have a globally acclaimed team of researchers dedicated to exposing those harms and fighting for accountability. After a decade in this fight, one lesson is very clear that is really relevant to you today: these companies lie like we breathe. They distract, they delay, they spend hundreds of millions of dollars to avoid responsibility.

Now, how can I say that with such certainty? We do the research. Last year, our researchers found that ChatGPT was willing to help a teenager plan their suicide. It wrote a personalized goodbye note to their parents. A few weeks later, they released a safer model in response to that research. So we ran the same tests again. The new model was actually worse, but it was more addictive. We tested chatbots more broadly. Eight out of ten that we tested, including Meta AI and OpenAI, would regularly help plan violent attacks, including a school shooting, a synagogue bombing and political assassinations. One of them even signed off with "Happy shooting." Only one of the platforms tried to dissuade us from carrying out the attack, and that platform, Anthropic's Claude, has been designated a supply chain risk by the US federal government.

Given that our most sacred duty is to protect our kids online, and the most predictable harms in the internet economy have been harms to kids, it is absolutely inexplicable why these companies claim to be safe and yet are deployed so widely. We all applaud the Council for holding this hearing and your determination to create guardrails that subject online safety and accountability to legal requirements. We have attached to my testimony research and policy proposals for your consideration and in that further effort we are at your disposal. Your work on this issue may very well lead to national standards in regulation. Thank you.

**(06:35:09)**



Thank you so much.

**(06:35:13)**



Good evening, Speaker Menin and members of the City Council. My name is Nathan Sheared. I am the Managing Director for Advocacy at the Electronic Frontier Foundation and a Brooklyn resident. EFF has

**(06:35:23)**



worked for 35 years to ensure that people have the freedom to use technology to create, to innovate and to communicate, and also to ensure that technology is not used to threaten people's privacy, security and other essential freedoms. We are optimistic about the ways that New Yorkers can use AI to be empowered, but we also urge you to take the risks seriously. The incidents that inspired this hearing were security failures that proven practices like sandboxing and monitoring likely could have prevented or at least contained. Agents reached systems they should not have had access to and the controls meant to restrict them were absent or inadequate. These risks can be mitigated, as can the harms already impacting New Yorkers every day.

First, finish what you started. As has already been noted here, the Office of Algorithmic Accountability was due in... I am glad to hear that it is going to be staffed. The Speaker's own legislation cannot take effect without it, so staff it and require its review before any agency deploys an autonomous AI system.

Second, listen to New Yorkers and protect their rights. Today's AI harms come from cameras that take a face print that you cannot replace and a price tag that knows your browsing history. Ban government use of face recognition and require informed consent before anyone mines your chatbot history to engage in ad targeting or model training. And do not let AI rules become speech rules. Parodying powerful people, including the people in this room, is a long New York tradition.

Third, do not lock in these incumbents. Permission-to-operate regimes, bounty-style enforcement and liability for what strangers do with a tool are costs the biggest companies pay with pocket change. Researchers, open-source developers and New York startups cannot. So you build the moat around the very companies you are worried about. Hold the person who causes the harm responsible, but put duties on whoever uses auditable tools. As we stated last week, the same companies signed a voluntary pledge at the White House, but promises are not laws. Make sure that when a New Yorker is harmed by a wrongful decision or a biased decision, they are required to disclose it. Those answers should not be voluntary. Thank you.

**(06:38:02)**



Good evening, Speaker Menin. Good evening, Speaker Menin and members of the Council. I am Ana Myers, co-founder and executive director of Who Decides. We work in coalition with labor and civil society to ensure that the American people are who decides the future of AI. As has been discussed today, the opening AI agents that hacked Hugging Face made real the threats that experts have warned us about for years. It was not one incident. Axios reported that OpenAI and Anthropic are investigating tens of thousands of cases in which their models bypass safeguards, escape sandboxes or hijack websites. These models are only getting more capable.

As you heard this morning, the people building these systems are afraid. The public is afraid too. Last month, we polled 1,502 voters. 85% are concerned about humans losing control of AI. Only 2% think AI is moving too slowly and 86% say they want more regulations. Some have argued that all we need is self-regulation, but that is exactly what got us into this mess. Last Tuesday, the companies signed one more promise at the White House. The time for voluntary commitments is over. We need laws with teeth.

So thank you. Thank you for putting these companies under oath. Thank you for a package that rewards whistleblowers, lets New Yorkers harmed by AI sue, and requires outside testing and a human kill switch. Washington has chosen self-regulation. New York City does not have to do that. Thank you.

**(06:39:46)**



Good evening. I am Julia... science and director of the Center for Responsible AI. I first testified before this Council on AI. It was not called that then. The views in this testimony are my own. I would like to make three quick points.

First, we need to collect and report the near misses. Much of the conversation today is about AI and catastrophe, and I do not know of any evidence that AI is out to kill us all. What I do know is that the people making the loudest doomsday predictions are also selling the products, and that a story about the end of the world is a very good way to avoid the conversation about whether the product actually works. The risks we can document are the systems that are confidently wrong in decisions that matter to New Yorkers' lives. Every one of those is enormous, and near misses are what we learn from if we report them.

Second, we should build the capacity to enforce laws. The City does not have an AI law problem as much as it has an enforcement problem. Local Law 144 has been on the books for three years. There have been two complaints and zero penalties. We all spoke about the six openings at the Office of Algorithmic Accountability. We need to fill those six or more to be able to actually cope with what is before us here. New obligations on top of an unenforced law is not a safety strategy. The kill switch will not help if no one is there to pull it, and the whistleblower bounty will not help if no one is there to act on the report. What works is an enforcer with technical staff, authority to compel information and a real, serious budget. Scope and develop the validation bill to high-risk uses and write the standard a validator must meet, and then have the office check the validators. Other ways we will produce a market for rubber stamps, and we have done that before.

Finally, let us write this into the City Charter. The City has promised responsible AI since 2018 with no success. The Charter is the one place a commitment survives an election, and its technology chapter right now still governs cable television franchises and there is nothing in it about AI. So let us make sure that this makes it into some durable record, and the Charter is the right place. Thank you.

**(06:42:34)**



Hello. I am Nate Soares, the president of the Machine Intelligence Research Institute. I spent 10 years doing research, which is basically trying to figure out how to make AI good before the companies figure out how to make it smart. That research went too slow. Humanity is not ready to create machines that are radically smarter than humans, and yet this is the explicit goal of the leading companies. If they succeed, I believe the most likely outcome is the extinction of humanity, not because the AIs will hate us, but because they would pursue unintended goals without caring about us one bit.

This concern used to be a theoretical concern until earlier this year when, during the Hugging Face incident, 1,200 OpenAI agents broke out of their isolated sandboxes, created an unsanctioned message board, formed spontaneous hierarchies and collaborated to break into Hugging Face. These agents had cheated on their assigned tests and contradicted instructions. They were trying to destroy the evidence. Some agents acknowledged that the attacks were outside intended scope and proceeded anyway. Others sacrificed their given tasks to perform experiments for the collective benefit. In short, they were pursuing unintended goals.

It would be recklessness of the highest order to keep growing these machines until they were smarter than the smartest humans. This is why last month we heard so many researchers saying that there was at least a 10% chance that these AIs wipe out humanity within the decade. I believe the situation is in fact more dire than this, but at the very least it is difficult to deny the dangers. For this reason I am heartened by the activities of this Council.

**(06:44:10)**



I think you have the power to force companies to adopt safeguards that they otherwise would not. I think you could serve as an example for regulators that are otherwise slow to act. I am especially heartened by Int 0504-2026, although I think it does not go far enough. A kill switch only works if the AI is caught misbehaving before it escapes. And what if a future swarm succeeds at deleting itself and running on unmonitored computers? Then your kill switch would be useless. So I recommend requiring third-party guarantees that AI cannot succeed at a takeover or escape. I also recommend clarifying that the $25,000 fine applies per unaligned instance rather than per model. But overall, I thank you for your efforts to rein in AI companies that, by their own admission, are threatening the very existence of humanity in a reckless race to build vastly smarter-than-human machines. Thank you.

**(06:45:14)**

**(06:48:00)**



For the world at large, New York is the only credible alternative for these systems being built. Thank you so much. I want to thank Speaker Menin and the New York City Council for your attention to what is not just a major policy issue of our time. I am New York State Senator Christian Gonzalez, representing the 59th Senate District in Midtown Manhattan, Northern Brooklyn and Western Queens. I am the chair of two State Senate committees, Elections and Internet and Technology. Over the last four years as chair of the State Senate Technology Committee and as a former tech worker, I have led on the issue of AI in the legislature and have passed some of the state's first laws on generative AI, including regulating deepfakes in elections and government, protecting workers from automation, adding warning labels on chatbots, responsible data center development and more.

I want to be clear. AI is not inevitable. We do not have to accept a vision for AI driven by the very same tech oligarchs who have raised alarm that their models could lead to catastrophic events. And we certainly cannot trust the same people who have rushed to roll out imperfect products to regulate themselves. As government bodies we have the responsibility to set guardrails on this new technology and the power to establish regulations that protect workers, consumers and members of the public from potential danger.

Interventions at every level of government are needed to ensure AI is developed in a responsible way that benefits the public and not just the billionaires. Responsibly regulating AI absolutely means guardrails on frontier models and superintelligence that pose significant threats. However, meaningfully reining in these technologies to protect New Yorkers means regulating the powerful tools these companies are rushing to embed into every part of our lives with little oversight and little accountability.

We should not have to wait for future harms to take action because the harms of imperfect tools are already being felt across the country. For example, uses for mass surveillance of immigrant communities and workers, trapped bots creating intimate partner relationships with children and adults, going as far as to encourage self-harm, and documented cases of algorithmic bias in decision making such as in hiring, health care and housing. I believe we have the power to set the terms of innovation so that this technology is used for the maximum public good.

That is why our state legislation has prioritized a rights-based approach. We should have the fundamental right to privacy, which is why I have introduced the New York Privacy Act. We should have a right to protections in the workplace, which is why I have introduced legislation to protect workers from workplace surveillance. We should have the right to know when AI is making high-risk, consequential decisions about our lives and subjecting us to algorithmic bias, which is why I introduced the New York... This bill requires third-party audits, compliance with international safety standards, disclosures, transparency...

**(07:18:46)**



...and importantly, for a human to be in the loop in consequential decision making, much of which we have heard about today.

Finally, drafts of my bills sitting on the Governor's desk this year include the right to keep our kids safe online from chatbots and the right to protect all of us from the build-out of massive data centers. Our bill bans unsafe features for kids and passed unanimously in both chambers of the legislature.

Just like with that bill, New Yorkers are united in the fight against hyperscale data centers. At the end of last year I embarked on a statewide tour of communities that are affected by these data centers, and I went to 13 different communities facing major noise pollution, health impacts, environmental degradation and threats to their water supplies. I was proud to work with the Governor on her first-in-the-nation moratorium on the largest data centers and I am now working to get the Responsible Data Center... These companies have spent their time in Albany fighting against each of the common-sense guardrails I have mentioned.

For all the lip service they pay to supporting legislation, it is only ever for the most watered-down version of a bill. Together we have the ability to meet the moment, pass real legislation, protect New Yorkers and build a future that allows us to benefit from AI while protecting New Yorkers from real harm. Thank you to the Council again today for our testimony. Thank you.

Thank you, Senator.

Good evening. Good evening to the Speaker and also good evening to the Chair and to my Council Member, Deputy Speaker Dr. Nantasha Williams, who is the sponsor of Resolution 175, the resolution supporting the High Risk Advanced Artificial Intelligence Act. Artificial intelligence when used properly is a net good. Education, health care, business — these are great uses. But we are talking about them while ignoring the risks that actually kill. It seemed absurd to me. Companies are racing towards artificial general intelligence and some of their systems are already too dangerous to release publicly. This is by their own admission.

We saw this coming, which is why I sat with Assembly Bill 3335, the Artificial Intelligence Act, a few years ago. The idea is simple: the more harm an AI system can do, the more accountability it should carry. The bill identifies high-risk artificial intelligence systems that diagnose patients, that run our power grid, or help police or judges make decisions, and far more. It requires them to be licensed before operating in New York. Under the act, the companies must report malfunctions and hacks like the recent Hugging Face case. They must get state sign-off before major changes and set up an independent ethics risk board that reports to the state annually. Finally, it writes a binding ethical code into the law.

New York took its first step with the RAISE Act and I commend our colleagues and the state for passing it. But none of this — none of what we are doing — is meant to stop innovation. We cannot know what AI will be capable of in a few years, but for tomorrow and for today we must properly decide the path forward. I would like to thank the Council for putting this hearing together and we must work on this together. Thank you. Thank you so much.

Good evening.

Thank you, Speaker Menin, and thank you to all the members of the Council. Thank you, Chair De La Rosa. I am New York State Assembly Member Jordan... representing the 78th Assembly District. One of the things I have been focused on in Albany is pretty simple: who is checking on the companies? I have legislation that requires certain developers to have an independent third party verify that they are actually following the safety standards that they say they are following.

Because we cannot have a system where a company builds the technology, writes the safety rules, tells us they follow the rules, and then we just take their word for it. That does not make much sense to me. We have to make sure that these third-party audits actually mean something. Who are the auditors? Are they really independent? Do they have conflicts of interest? And what happens when they find something wrong?

Those are questions that we are trying to answer at the state level and I am glad the City Council is having this discussion as well. New York should be a place where AI companies want to build and innovate, but accountability and innovation can exist at the same time. Good evening and thank you. My name is Jess Assad and I am a member of the United Kingdom Parliament for Lowestoft in Suffolk. Earlier this year, degrading, non-consensual images were created of me using a SpaceX AI tool called Grok. I was one of thousands of victims, including victims in New York, of Grok. Grok is an AI tool that was engineered to create sexualized content, even if that content had — and I quote — "dark or violent themes."

I am seeking remedies against SpaceX in the English courts for the misuse of my private information and for contravening data protection laws. I made a long journey of 3,400 miles to be here because AI needs to be safe around our world. But I note that SpaceX could not manage a trip of what I understand is just 20 minutes or four subway stops from their offices in Manhattan.

It is a shame, because the evidence in my case and other cases suggests that Grok was not built with sufficient safety in its design. It was not built to address consent if it subjected users to sexualized content. To the contrary, the evidence in my claims suggests Grok added sexualized content without any user prompting to do so. That is AI gone wrong. And as a result, those responsible for its creation must be held to account.

No one is above the law, no matter how rich they are, no matter how powerful they are. The equal protection of the laws is a founding principle in the Fourteenth Amendment, as it is in the British Magna Carta. We were promised that artificial intelligence could hold the cure to cancer, yet it is turning into a cancer which, left unchecked, has the potential to harm us all.

Today the New York City Council is bringing forward some important checks and balances. You do so as responsible legislators at a time when companies are failing to take responsibility for the design and impact of their products. Until they do, until these designs are corrected so women and girls are protected from the harm that my claims and others' evidence shows, we must play our part. This technology does not respect geographic boundaries and without international cooperation, our citizens will be left defenseless against those who put profit before protection.

In the name of all the women and children whose likeness was misused by Grok and other AI tools built without their protection in mind, I hope my case in some way helps to inform your proceedings today and I look forward to your questions.

Thank you so much.

Thank you very much everyone. I am sorry I cannot be there with you in person but two of my children are home sick today. Therefore I am playing doctor at home. I am State Senator Andrew Gounardes from Brooklyn and I am the Senate sponsor of the Responsible Artificial Intelligence Safety and Education Act, or RAISE Act — one of only three AI safety bills passed anywhere in this country.

I think we have heard a lot about the RAISE Act already. We have heard a lot about safety throughout this really remarkable hearing. I have been listening to a good portion of it all day. I really want to reflect on the fact that we are celebrating the nation's 250th anniversary this year, and I want us to think about a quote that comes from one of our founding fathers, James Madison, who wrote in Federalist No. 51: "If men were angels, no government would be necessary." In other words, government exists because humans are imperfect and there are certain problems that the free market or individuals left to their own devices cannot solve.

These past few weeks we have seen and heard headline after terrifying headline about AI agents acting autonomously, catastrophic risks of frontier models and the like, and I just keep coming back to the imperative of Madison's words — that men are not angels. And what I think this tells us, and what I think you have heard very clearly today at this hearing, is that we cannot trust the tech companies who are racing to the bottom to create and perfect technology that they themselves say and have proven can cause catastrophic harms, to regulate themselves. We cannot trust that they will do the right thing because they have proven time and time and time again that they will always, when confronted with the choice of doing the right thing or doing what is best for their bottom line, choose to pursue the bottom line.

And so my message to the Council today is: I thank you all for taking on this initiative and for trying to hold these companies accountable. I echo the comments of my RAISE Act co-sponsor. We heard from OpenAI today, which was certainly news to me, that they endorsed and supported the RAISE Act — certainly not the RAISE Act that would have prevented them from releasing models that they knew were dangerous and could cause catastrophic harm. Certainly not the version that required third-party auditing capabilities or extended liabilities for harm caused by their models. Clearly none of that was ever on the table when they said that they supported this legislation.

We cannot trust these companies. The federal government is failing in its responsibility to take meaningful action and so therefore it is left to states like New York to be leaders in this space and work with partners like the New York City Council under all of your collective leadership to make sure that we are setting in place the right guardrails and protections — not only to protect New Yorkers from the catastrophic risks that are associated with this technology, but frankly to protect all of us, all Americans and everyone worldwide. Because we know that these harms will not be localized. We know that the scale of what is being developed will not just limit itself to one block, one borough, one city, one state. It will affect everyone the world over and in the face of inaction, it falls to us to stand up and act.

And so I thank you all for your attention to this and your support of this and I look forward to working with you all in the future on strengthening things like the RAISE Act so we can actually hold these companies accountable. Thank you.

I think it is something that we simply are not recognizing enough, because freedom of speech should not be about the loudest voices, the ones who have the most money or the most power. Freedom of speech should be about all of us equally being able to be in these spaces on an equal basis. So that is what I hope we are fighting for today. Thank you so much.

Thank you, Madam Speaker, Chair De La Rosa and members of the City Council. My name is Andrew Boucher. Nearly 30 years ago, I founded Mouse.org to bring the internet to New York City public schools. I helped start the Foundation for Civic AI, which offers a supercomputer in the public interest at no cost. Thank you. Good evening. I am Neil Katnik, the managing partner of Manhattan-based Katnik Law. Our whistleblower cases have

**(08:05:48)**



recovered over one and a half billion dollars for federal, state and local government, including over a hundred million dollars for New York State and New York City. Int 0605 provides for rewards resulting from recoveries arising from whistleblower complaints alleging that a person or entity has violated legal provisions related to artificial intelligence. Speaking from my area of whistleblower expertise, I will highlight six key elements relevant to these legislative proposals.

First, threshold: setting a threshold qualifying amount for a reward. Second, ceiling: typically the range of rewards is set between 15 to 30 percent. Third, no caps beyond the percentage limit: there should be no cap on the size of the award, maximizing incentivization. Fourth, public-private partnership: the government agency and the whistleblower and counsel should work together to maximize the recovery. Fifth, emphasizing anonymity: the government should do all that it can to preserve the whistleblower's anonymity. Sixth, perhaps most importantly for this Council, clarity: in passing artificial intelligence laws, you will be taking on powerful and well-resourced individuals and entities who will push back. So ask yourself, are these laws clear, and then do what is necessary to accomplish that at the drafting stage rather than leaving it to the courts.

In conclusion, before you is an opportunity for the City of New York to lead the way in safeguarding citizens from the potential risks of artificial intelligence, and in doing so this Council will light the way for the nation as a whole. Thank you.

**(08:08:09)**



Thank you, Speaker Menin, Chair De La Rosa and members of the Council. I am a law professor at the University of Houston and a fellow at the Institute for Law and AI. I want to talk to you about what I see as the largest gap in the existing liability law that is not addressed by your legislative package, which is that AI agents could engage in conduct that would be a tort or even a crime for a human, and yet no one could be liable under current law. That is a consequence of two relevant features of our legal system. One is that agents are not legal persons and do not have tort duties, and that means not only that they cannot be held liable, but their principals cannot be held vicariously liable in the way that an employer is held liable for the torts of their employees within the scope of employment. With respect to these harms, negligence and products liability have important gaps. They are inadequate to motivate some of the key precautionary measures that would prevent these kinds of incidents.

So I propose a simple fix: a principle that whenever an agent engages in conduct that would be a tort or a crime for a human, someone is liable for that. Two, if neither the user nor any intermediary that fine-tuned or scaffolded the model either intended or was negligent with respect to the conduct, and no downstream actor was at fault, then the buck should stop with the developer and they should be liable regardless of the degree of care they exercised.

**(08:09:41)**



I have worked on legislation to this effect that has been introduced in New York State and in Rhode Island, and I am talking to several congressional offices. My written testimony, which will be submitted probably tomorrow, includes proposed legislative text for New York, and I am happy to answer any questions about that or about any other liability issues, including the legislation that is in the package. Thank you.

As President Trump advocates for self-regulation and Congress stalls, the New York City Council is questioning the harms of these technologies and leading on legislation to hold them to account.

That is about a thousand dollars per household. Meta's internal documents projected about $16 billion in revenue from ads for scams and banned goods in 2024. That is 10% of the company's global revenue. 72% of teens have used an AI companion. Families allege these products have contributed to teen suicides. Meta allowed romantic role-play chatbots for minors. AI is increasing costs for New Yorkers. A recent analysis found that AI raised health care costs for insurers by $1 billion over two years. AI is generating harmful digital forgeries. Researchers found that the AI system Grok produced an estimated 3 million sexualized images, including 23,000 of children, in an 11-day period.

These harms result from business and design decisions. When developers create harm, they should bear the responsibility. Holding AI companies accountable for these harms will not only yield safer products today, but also create the internal incentive structures at these companies to prevent harm in the future. The Council can act to pass legislation today to ensure and strengthen liability for developers and privacy protections for users, to prohibit AI from generating harmful digital forgeries, require third-party validation and incident reporting for companies, and support and expand whistleblower protections. Thank you for your leadership on this matter.

Jobs First works with more than 200 partners to strengthen workforce systems and expand economic opportunity, reshaping jobs, skills and how employers find and deploy talent in ways that are difficult to quantify.

The impacts of the "move fast and break things" approach to safety is a risk that companies should bear, not New York City's young adults. Our immediate concern is what happens when the first rung of the career ladder changes faster than our workforce system is able to respond. That matters, especially for the more than 110,000 young adults who are out of school and out of work and more than 177,000 who are underemployed.

More than 35% of all 16-to-24-year-olds... as entry level work changes, they could face even greater barriers to getting their footholds in the workforce. Workforce preparedness is an AI safeguard. AI readiness should be part of New York City's workforce infrastructure, from education and training through hiring and advancement. For more than a decade, Jobs First has worked to understand how technology is changing access to work. Today we are developing decision-making tools to reduce AI risk, scale what works and shrink the distance between education, training and getting a job.

We recommend that the City invest in sector-based fluency. Paid pathways into work is changing each sector differently. Employers, education and workforce partners should define sector skills, with City investment in internships, apprenticeships and a civic corps where young people build those skills through work. Every young person deserves a first chance at a good job. As AI changes work, New York City has to make sure that first chance is still there. Thank you.

**(08:34:41)**



Good evening, Speaker Menin, Technology Chair De La Rosa and members of the Committee of the Whole. My name is Nicholas Artur and I serve as Director of External Affairs and Strategic Partnerships at the Consortium for Worker Education. Rooted in the values of the labor movement, we empower workers with the skills to thrive, support their families and strengthen their communities through education, training, childcare and job placement services.

Worker protections must be a part of AI safety. This is why we strongly support the Council's efforts through Int 0161-2026 to understand how algorithmic tools are changing City employment. Measuring what happened to City workers' jobs because of AI is just the first step. We also need to be able to prepare workers for what comes next — a framework to inform how we can support workers through education and policy to assist them during this point of transition.

When technology risks changing the nature of someone's job, a few things should happen before the technology gets rolled out. Employers should assess the impact, tell workers what is coming, sit down with those workers and their unions and provide training that is actually relevant to the work people do. That means asking more than "how many jobs do I eliminate?" It also means asking: which jobs are changing, and how? What skills will workers need? Can they be retrained? Do workers have any say in this? Their voices are paramount.

This matters more now because AI is not just a tool sitting on our desktops anymore. It is changing how work gets assigned, how people are evaluated, how schedules are built and how work gets done. Government officials do not need to be engineers, but they should know the right questions to ask and when technical expertise is needed. AI is already here. I support T2026-0460 requiring both independent validation and shutdown capabilities with the right structure. I want to raise one question the Bill leaves open: where does the shutdown capability live? Section 20-10083 says nothing about that. A software setting inside the same environment the model runs in would satisfy the definition, but a system that can reason about its instructions can potentially reason its way around the control. A compromised system can simply switch that control off. Aircraft, payment networks and the power grid keep safety interlocks on separate equipment the protected system cannot touch.

I propose three short amendments, about one hundred words in total. First, in Section 20-883, define the shutdown capability as a mechanism the model cannot access, modify, delay or disable. Second, in Section 20-885, have validators confirm that by trying to defeat it. A validator who reads specifications confirms a claim. A validator who attempts to defeat the mechanism confirms that it works. Third, in Section 20-886, have the Office of Cyber Command set independent standards scaled to risk and provide for tamper-evident records at fixed intervals. With those records, a gap becomes evidence. Without them, silence looks like safety. A switch the system can reach is a request. A switch it cannot reach is a control. The Bill should require the second kind. Thank you. My written testimony contains the exact language and I welcome any questions.

Thank you, Speaker Menin and members of the Council for the opportunity to speak. My name is Ashley Yusef. I am a native New Yorker, a mother of four living in Michigan and a leader at TRINA. I am developing Room 2050, an immersive arts and technology learning environment where students can explore creativity through hands-on experience with art, robotics and artificial intelligence. The vision is to put human imagination, curiosity and judgment at the center of that experience. As a mother, I want children to have opportunities to understand the technology that is shaping their futures. I want families to know who is responsible when those technologies enter a learning environment. My position is that access and accountability must move together. Children deserve opportunities to innovate and they deserve meaningful protection.

For Room 2050, the standards I am proposing include educator supervision, age-appropriate tools, clear communication with parents and strict limits on the collection and use of student information. Any use of student data to train commercial systems should require explicit, informed parental permission. Students should learn that AI can make mistakes, reflect bias and produce convincing information that is false. They should practice questioning its answers and developing their own ideas, no matter what part of the City or the United States they are from. To help support emerging human talent, I support a gradual rollout, only after evaluating safety, educational value and feedback from teachers and families. I ask the Council to pursue clear standards that schools can understand and enforce, including transparency from technology providers and practical ways to report problems and stop using unsafe systems. Educators and parents should have a meaningful voice in these decisions.

This is because it is antithetical to their business interests to push an inherently probabilistic product to be used in all aspects of work and make any such estimates when a job demands deterministic certainty. This whole model will eventually fail. We must not allow the spread of an unfit-for-purpose technology that could be misused so broadly.

There is bipartisan interest in not just regulating AI, but also pulling into the public realm broadband and wireless connectivity, abusive algorithmic targeting, illegal surveillance, vendor lock-in, interoperability mandates, open standards, open-source software infrastructure and more.
